From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Meat from dogs used as food

This article is about the human consumption of dogs. For meat eaten by dogs, see [Dog food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_food "Dog food"). For the video game character, see [Dogmeat (Fallout)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogmeat_(Fallout) "Dogmeat (Fallout)").

Dog meat

**Dog meat**, also known as **fragrant meat**, or simply **fragrant**,[&#8202;](#ref-back-4-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-4)[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-5) is the [meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meat "Meat") derived from [dogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog "Dog"). Historically, human consumption of dog meat has been recorded in many parts of the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-6)

In the 21st century, dog meat is consumed to a limited extent in [Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodia "Cambodia"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-7) [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wingfield-Hayes222-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-Wingfield-Hayes222) [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam "Vietnam"), parts of [Northeastern India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India "Northeast India"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-:12) [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia "Indonesia"), [Ghana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghana "Ghana"), [Laos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laos "Laos"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-10) [Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria "Nigeria"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCNigeria2-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-BBCNigeria2) [Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea "Korea")[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-:5)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-:0)[&#8202;](#ref-back-안용근1-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-안용근1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Podberscek2009-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-Podberscek2009) and [Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland "Switzerland").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:62-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-:62) In these areas, the legality of dog meat consumption varies with some nations permitting it or lacking a nationwide ban. It was estimated in 2014 that worldwide, 25 million dogs were eaten each year by humans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cz2-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-Cz2)\[ _[needs update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_\]

Some cultures view the consumption of dog meat as part of their traditional, ritualistic, or day-to-day [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine "Cuisine"), and other cultures consider consumption of dog meat a [taboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_taboo "Food taboo"), even where it had been consumed in the past. Opinions also vary drastically across different regions within different countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Times19402-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-Times19402)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mawson2-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-Mawson2)

## Historical practices

### Americas

#### Aztecs

In the [Aztec Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztec_Empire "Aztec Empire"), [Mexican hairless dogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_hairless_dog "Mexican hairless dog") were bred for many purposes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-About_THE_XOLOITZCUINTLE-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-About_THE_XOLOITZCUINTLE) [Hernán Cortés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hern%C3%A1n_Cort%C3%A9s "Hernán Cortés"), an enemy of the Aztec, claimed in a letter that when he arrived in [Tenochtitlan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenochtitlan "Tenochtitlan") in 1519, "small gelded dogs which they breed for eating" were among the goods sold in the city markets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-21) No other source corroborates this practice. These dogs, _[Xoloitzcuintles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xoloitzcuintle "Xoloitzcuintle")_, were often depicted in [pre-Columbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Columbian "Pre-Columbian") Mexican pottery. The breed was almost extinct in the 1940s, but the British military attaché in Mexico City, Norman Wright, developed a thriving breed from some of the dogs he found in remote villages.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-:7) The genetic heritage of the breed has been almost erased through interbreeding with other dog breeds to keep its looks alive.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-23)

#### Native North Americans

The traditional culture surrounding the consumption of dog meat varied from [tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe "Tribe") to tribe among the original inhabitants of North America, with some tribes relishing it as a [delicacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delicacy "Delicacy"), and others (such as the [Comanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comanche "Comanche")) treating it as a forbidden food.[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-24) Native peoples of the [Great Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Plains "Great Plains"), such as the [Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sioux "Sioux") and [Cheyenne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheyenne "Cheyenne"), consumed it, but there was a concurrent religious [taboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taboo "Taboo") against the meat of wild canines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-25)

The [Kickapoo people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kickapoo_people "Kickapoo people") include puppy meat in many of their traditional festivals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-26) This practice has been well documented in the [Works Progress Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Works_Progress_Administration "Works Progress Administration") "Indian Pioneer History Project for Oklahoma".[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-27)[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-28)

On 20 December 2018, the federal [Dog and Cat Meat Trade Prohibition Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_and_Cat_Meat_Trade_Prohibition_Act_of_2018 "Dog and Cat Meat Trade Prohibition Act of 2018") was signed into law as part of the [2018 Farm Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_United_States_farm_bill "2018 United States farm bill"). It bans slaughtering dogs and cats for food in the United States, with exceptions for Native American rituals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-29)

### Europe

[Ovid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovid "Ovid"), [Plutarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutarch "Plutarch"), [Pliny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder "Pliny the Elder"), and other Latin authors, describe the sacrifice of puppies ( _catulina_) to infernal deities, and for protection against [grain-rust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rust_(fungus) "Rust (fungus)"), the meat being subsequently prepared and consumed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-30)

### Austronesia

See also: [Domesticated plants and animals of Austronesia §Canis lupus familiaris (dog)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domesticated_plants_and_animals_of_Austronesia#Canis_lupus_familiaris_(dog) "Domesticated plants and animals of Austronesia")

#### Indigenous Taiwanese

The [Indigenous Taiwanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Taiwanese "Indigenous Taiwanese") originally had [taboos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taboo "Taboo") against eating dogs, which featured prominently in their cultural myths. The indigenous [Taiwan Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Dog "Taiwan Dog") was deeply valued as a hunting companion. However, they also started consuming dog meat after it was introduced by incoming [waishengren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waishengren "Waishengren") (mainlander) [Han Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese "Han Chinese") migrants in the mid-20th century, leading to the near-extinction of the Taiwan Dog.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wright-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-Wright)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cook-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-Cook)

#### Maritime Southeast Asia

Domesticated dogs were carried into the Philippines by the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic "Neolithic") [Austronesian migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_expansion "Austronesian expansion") from [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Taiwanese "Indigenous Taiwanese"), whose dogs in turn were acquired from the pre-Austronesian cultures in [Neolithic southeastern China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_China "Neolithic China"). Dog meat were consumed in some pre-colonial Philippine ethnic groups during certain [shamanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babaylan "Babaylan") rituals and special occasions. However, dog bones are very rarely found in [middens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midden "Midden") in archaeological sites, in contrast to pig and deer remains; and most complete dog remains in archaeological sites are of dog burials near or beside human graves. This indicates that while dogs were sometimes eaten, they were primarily kept as companions and hunting dogs, and not as food animals. The Spanish historian [Francisco Ignacio Alcina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Ignacio_Alcina "Francisco Ignacio Alcina") have detailed descriptions of the affectionate treatment of dogs by native [Visayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visayans "Visayans") in the 17th century, during the early [Spanish colonial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonial_period_of_the_Philippines "Spanish colonial period of the Philippines"). Alcina disapprovingly records that dogs were treated by locals like their own children. Thus, the rise in the consumption of [dog meat as food in the secular context](#Philippines) in some ethnic groups in the Philippines is likely to have only occurred some time after the arrival of the Spanish.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ochoa-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-Ochoa)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Agoot-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-Agoot)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Alfonso-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-Alfonso)

The tradition of eating dog meat for ritual purposes in some ethnic groups survived into the modern times in the Cordillera highlands of the Philippines. Among [Cordillerans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igorot_people "Igorot people"), dogs are sacrificed and eaten in a cleansing ritual known as _dao-es_ or _daw-es_. The ritual is typically done after a person dies unexpectedly (through murder or an accident), gets severely ill, was released from prison, or witnessed death firsthand. Cordillerans believe that dogs have one of the purest souls among animals, comparable to humans, and thus they are the most appealing to the ancestor spirits ( _[anito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anito "Anito")_). The dog soul is sent as a messenger to the spirit world by a shaman ( _[mambunong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mambunong "Mambunong")_), which is believed to cleanse the minds of participants of images of death and evil spirits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Agoot-1)[\[34\]](#refnote-Agoot)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ngalob-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-Ngalob)[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-37)[&#8202;](#ref-back-regan-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-regan) These rituals are still legally permitted, though they are required to keep records and are overseen by the Committee on Animal Welfare.[&#8202;](#ref-back-4paws-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-4paws)

#### Oceania

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Hawaiian_natives_wearing_kihei%2C_with_animals%2C_sketch_by_Louis_Choris.jpg/250px-Hawaiian_natives_wearing_kihei%2C_with_animals%2C_sketch_by_Louis_Choris.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hawaiian_natives_wearing_kihei,_with_animals,_sketch_by_Louis_Choris.jpg) Extinct [Hawaiian Poi Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Poi_Dog "Hawaiian Poi Dog") (center)

Dogs lost their economic importance as hunting animals (usually for wild boar) among Austronesians that reached the smaller islands in [Melanesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_Melanesia "Island Melanesia") and [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia "Polynesia"). They became a competitor for limited food resources and thus were themselves eaten. The Austronesian domesticated dogs originally carried by the [Lapita Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapita_Culture "Lapita Culture") migrations were eaten to extinction in many islands since ancient times. Dogs were reintroduced later on from surviving populations in other islands as well as dogs that descended from non-Austronesian [Mainland Southeast Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia "Mainland Southeast Asia") populations. This caused a marked discontinuity in the genes of domesticated dogs, as well as the terms for dogs, among Austronesians in the Pacific Islands, in comparison to other Austronesian regions in Island Southeast Asia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Blust-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-Blust)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Greig-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-Greig)

Dogs were historically eaten in [Tahiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahiti "Tahiti") and other islands of [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia "Polynesia"), including Hawaii[&#8202;](#ref-back-Titcomb_1969-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-Titcomb_1969)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ellis_1839-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-Ellis_1839) at the time of first European contact. [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook "James Cook"), when first visiting Tahiti in 1769, recorded in his journal, "few were there of us but what allow'd that a South Sea Dog was next to an English Lamb, one thing in their favour is that they live entirely upon Vegetables".[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-44) Calvin Schwabe reported in 1979 that dog was widely eaten in Hawaii and considered to be of higher quality than pork or chicken. When Hawaiians first encountered early British and American explorers, they were at a loss to explain the visitors' attitudes about dog meat. The Hawaiians raised both dogs and pigs as pets and for food. They could not understand why their British and American visitors only found the pig suitable for consumption.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchwabe1979[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidSiBntk9jGmoCpgPA168_168]-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchwabe1979[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidSiBntk9jGmoCpgPA168_168]) This practice seems to have died out, along with the native Hawaiian breed of dog, the unique [Hawaiian Poi Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Poi_Dog "Hawaiian Poi Dog"), which was primarily used for this purpose.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-46)

Although Hawaii has outlawed commercial sales of dog meat, until the federal [Dog and Cat Meat Trade Prohibition Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_and_Cat_Meat_Trade_Prohibition_Act_of_2018 "Dog and Cat Meat Trade Prohibition Act of 2018") it was legal to slaughter an animal classified as a pet if it was "bred for human consumption" and done in a "humane" manner. This allowed dog meat trade to continue, mostly using stray, lost, or stolen dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-47)[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-48)

## Religious dietary laws

According to [kashrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashrut "Kashrut"), Jewish dietary law, it is forbidden to consume the flesh of terrestrial mammals that do not chew their cud and have cloven hooves, which includes dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-49)

In [Islamic dietary laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_dietary_laws "Islamic dietary laws"), the consumption of the flesh of a dog, or any carnivorous animal, or any animal bearing fangs, claws, fingers or reptilian scales, is considered [haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram "Haram") ("prohibited"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-50) whereas the Maliki school deems it [makruh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makruh "Makruh") ("disliked but not prohibited").[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-51)

## Dogs as survival food

### Wars and famines

In most European countries, the consumption of dog meat is taboo. Exceptions occurred in times of scarcity, such as sieges or famines.

In Germany, dog meat has been eaten in every major crisis since at least the time of [Frederick the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_the_Great "Frederick the Great"), and was commonly referred to as "blockade mutton".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Times19402-1)[\[18\]](#refnote-Times19402)

During the 19th century westward movement in the United States, _mountainmen_, [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas "Indigenous peoples of the Americas"), the [U.S. Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Army "U.S. Army"), as well as the [Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America "Confederate States of America") during the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War")[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-52) frequently had to sustain themselves on dog meat; the first to be consumed would be the horses, then the mules, and lastly the dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-53)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Grande_Boucherie_Canine_a_Paris_%28canular_photographique%29.jpg/250px-Grande_Boucherie_Canine_a_Paris_%28canular_photographique%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grande_Boucherie_Canine_a_Paris_(canular_photographique).jpg) Photographic hoax showing the announced opening of a Great Dog Butchery, Paris, France, 1910

During the [Siege of Paris (1870–1871)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Paris_(1870%E2%80%931871) "Siege of Paris (1870–1871)"), food shortages caused by the [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire "German Empire") blockade of the city caused the citizens of Paris to turn to alternative sources for food, including dog meat. Dog meat was also reported as being sold by some butchers in Paris in 1910.[&#8202;](#ref-back-romi-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-romi)[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-55)

In the early 20th century, high meat prices led to widespread consumption of horse and dog meat in Germany.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Times1907-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-Times1907)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimes1900-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-nytimes1900)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimes1898-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-nytimes1898)

In the early 20th century in the United States, dog meat was consumed during times of meat shortage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimes1904-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-nytimes1904)

A few meat shops sold dog meat during the [German occupation of Belgium in World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_occupation_of_Belgium_during_World_War_I "German occupation of Belgium during World War I"), when food was scarce.[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-60)

In the latter part of [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I "World War I"), dog meat was being eaten in [Saxony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Saxony "Kingdom of Saxony") by the poorer classes because of famine conditions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimes1918-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-nytimes1918)

In Germany, the consumption of dog meat continued in the 1920s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimes1923-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-nytimes1923)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytime1925-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-nytime1925) In 1937, a meat inspection law targeted against [trichinella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trichinella "Trichinella") was introduced for pigs, dogs, [boars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boar "Boar"), foxes, [badgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badger "Badger"), and other carnivores.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-64)

During severe meat shortages coinciding with the [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany") occupation from 1940 to 1945, sausages found to have been made of dog meat were confiscated by Nazi authorities in the Netherlands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimes1940-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-nytimes1940)

### Expeditions and emergencies

Travelers sometimes have to eat their accompanying dogs to survive when stranded without other food. For example, [Benedict Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_Allen "Benedict Allen") ate his dog when lost in the Brazilian rainforest.[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-66) A case in Canada was reported in 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-67)

#### Lewis and Clark

During the [Lewis and Clark Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_and_Clark_Expedition "Lewis and Clark Expedition") (1803–1806), [Meriwether Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meriwether_Lewis "Meriwether Lewis") and the other members of the [Corps of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corps_of_Discovery "Corps of Discovery") consumed dog meat, either from their own animals or supplied by Native American tribes, including the [Paiutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paiute "Paiute") and Wah-clel-lah Indians, a branch of the [Watlatas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watlata "Watlata"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-68) the [Clatsop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clatsop "Clatsop"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-69) the [Teton Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakota_people "Lakota people") (Lakota),[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-70) the [Nez Perce Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nez_Perce_tribe "Nez Perce tribe") (who did not eat dog themselves[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-71)),[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-72) and the [Hidatsas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hidatsa "Hidatsa").[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-73) Lewis and the members of the expedition ate dog meat, except [William Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Clark_(explorer) "William Clark (explorer)"), who reportedly could not bring himself to eat dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-74)[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-75)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clarkson2013-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-Clarkson2013)

#### Polar exploration

British explorer [Ernest Shackleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Shackleton "Ernest Shackleton") and his [Imperial Trans-Antarctic Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Trans-Antarctic_Expedition "Imperial Trans-Antarctic Expedition") became trapped, and ultimately killed their sled dogs for food.

Norwegian explorer [Roald Amundsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roald_Amundsen "Roald Amundsen")'s party famously planned to eat their sled dogs, as well as to feed weaker dogs to other dogs, during their expedition to the South Pole. This allowed the party to carry less food, thus lightening the load, and ultimately helped Amundsen to win his race to the South Pole against [Robert Scott's expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terra_Nova_Expedition "Terra Nova Expedition"), which used ponies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-77) When comparing sled dogs to ponies as draught animals, Amundsen noted:

> There is the obvious advantage that dog can be fed on dog. One can reduce one's pack little by little, slaughtering the feebler ones and feeding the chosen with them. In this way they get fresh meat. Our dogs lived on dog's flesh and [pemmican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemmican "Pemmican") the whole way, and this enabled them to do splendid work. And if we ourselves wanted a piece of fresh meat we could cut off a delicate little fillet; it tasted to us as good as the best beef. The dogs do not object at all; as long as they get their share they do not mind what part of their comrade's carcass it comes from. All that was left after one of these canine meals was the teeth of the victim – and if it had been a really hard day, these also disappeared.[&#8202;](#ref-back-southpole-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-southpole)

[Douglas Mawson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Mawson "Douglas Mawson") and [Xavier Mertz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_Mertz "Xavier Mertz") were part of the [Far Eastern Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_Eastern_Party "Far Eastern Party"), a three-man sledging team with [Lieutenant B. E. S. Ninnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrave_Edward_Sutton_Ninnis "Belgrave Edward Sutton Ninnis"), to survey King George V Land, Antarctica. On 14 December 1912 Ninnis fell through a snow-covered crevasse along with most of the party's rations, and was never seen again. Mawson and Mertz turned back immediately. They had one and a half weeks' food for themselves and nothing at all for the dogs. Their meagre provisions forced them to eat their remaining sled dogs on their 315-mile (507km) return journey. Their meat was tough, stringy and without a vestige of fat. Each animal yielded very little, and the major part was fed to the surviving dogs, which ate the meat, skin and bones until nothing remained. The men also ate the dog's brains and livers. However, eating the liver of sled dogs produces the condition [hypervitaminosis A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypervitaminosis_A "Hypervitaminosis A") because canines have a much higher tolerance for vitamin A than humans do. Mertz suffered a quick deterioration. He developed stomach pains and became incapacitated and incoherent. On 7 January 1913, Mertz died. Mawson continued alone, eventually making it back to camp alive.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mawson2-1)[\[19\]](#refnote-Mawson2)
