Before 2018, it was banned in six states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-127) |  | ![Red X](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/ba/Red_x.svg/20px-Red_x.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)N Import illegal |

Laws about dog meat intended for human consumption

## Modern practices

### Africa

#### Cameroon

Dogs are eaten by [Vame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vame_language "Vame language") people for certain religious rituals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-128)

#### Democratic Republic of the Congo

In 2011, it was reported that, due to high prices on other types of meat, the consumption of dog meat is common despite a longstanding taboo.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-129)

#### Ghana

The [Tallensi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallensi "Tallensi"), the Akyims, the Kokis, and the Yaakuma, one of many cultures of Ghana, consider dog meat a delicacy. The [Mamprusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamprusi_people "Mamprusi people") people generally avoid dog meat, and it is eaten in a "courtship stew" provided by a king to his royal lineage. Two Tribes in Ghana, [Frafra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frafra_people "Frafra people") and [Dagaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dagaaba "Dagaaba") are particularly known to be "tribal playmates" and consumption of dog meat is the common bond between the two tribes. Every year around September, games are organised between these two tribes and the Dog Head is the trophy at stake for the winning tribe.[&#8202;](#ref-back-simoons1994p229-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-simoons1994p229)

It was reported in 2017 that increasing demand for dog meat (due to the belief it gives more energy) has led politician [Anthony Karbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Karbo "Anthony Karbo") to propose dog meat factories in three northern regions of Ghana.[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-131)[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-132)

#### Nigeria

Main article: [Dog meat consumption in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_meat_consumption_in_Nigeria "Dog meat consumption in Nigeria")

Dogs are eaten by various groups in some states of Nigeria, including Ondo State, Akwa Ibom, Cross River, Plateau, Kalaba, Taraba and Gombe of Nigeria.[&#8202;](#ref-back-simoons1994p229-1)[\[130\]](#refnote-simoons1994p229)[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-133) They are believed to have medicinal powers. The meat is believed to improve one's sex life, provide immunity from diseases and poisoning, and offer protection from [juju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juju "Juju") (charms).[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCNigeria2-1)[\[11\]](#refnote-BBCNigeria2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-134)

In late 2014, the fear of contracting the [Ebola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebola "Ebola") virus disease from [bushmeat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushmeat "Bushmeat") led at least one major Nigerian newspaper to imply that eating dog meat was a healthy alternative.[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-135) That paper documented a thriving trade in dog meat and slow sales of even well smoked bushmeat.

### Americas

#### Canada

It is legal to eat dog meat in Canada. According to a writer for the _[National Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Post "National Post")_, this is because it is not necessary to prevent people from eating dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-136) While it is technically legal to serve dog meat in a restaurant in Canada if it has been inspected, it may not be possible to actually do so.[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-137) In 2003, uninspected frozen canine meat was found at a Chinese restaurant named "Panda Garden" in [Edmonton, Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton,_Alberta "Edmonton, Alberta").[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-138) Provincial authorities determine if an animal species can be approved for slaughter; which would require dogs to be classified as a "food animal" and for individual animals to not be raised as pets. No license has ever been granted for this purpose. There is also no recognized way to import dog meat into the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-139)

#### United States

In December 2018, Donald Trump signed the [Dog and Cat Meat Trade Prohibition Act of 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_and_Cat_Meat_Trade_Prohibition_Act_of_2018 "Dog and Cat Meat Trade Prohibition Act of 2018") into law. Previously, the dog and cat meat trade was legal in 44 states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-140)

In July 2023, congresspeople [Vern Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vern_Buchanan "Vern Buchanan") and [Jared Moskowitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jared_Moskowitz "Jared Moskowitz") introduced a resolution that would call for banning dog and cat meat globally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-141)

### Asia/Pacific

#### Cambodia

Dog meat consumption is not practiced by most Cambodians. A new campaign began in 2020 to end dog meat consumption.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-142)[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-143)[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-144)[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-145)[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-146)[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-147)[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-148)[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-149)

#### Hong Kong

In Hong Kong, the _Dogs and Cats Ordinance_ was introduced by the British Hong Kong Government on 6 January 1950.[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-150) It prohibits the slaughter of any dog or cat for use as food by fine and imprisonment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-151)[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-152) In February 1998, a [Hong Konger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Konger "Hong Konger") was sentenced to one month imprisonment and a fine of two thousand HK dollars for hunting street dogs for food.[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-153) Four local men were sentenced to 30 days imprisonment in December 2006 for having slaughtered two dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-154)

#### India

Consumption of dog meat is seen in [Northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India "Northeast India"), especially in the states of [Mizoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizoram "Mizoram"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-155) [Nagaland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagaland "Nagaland"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-156)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Humane_Society_International-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-Humane_Society_International-2020) and [Manipur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manipur "Manipur"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-158) as well as in parts of [Meghalaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meghalaya "Meghalaya").[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-159)

In 2016, animal rights activist [Maneka Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maneka_Gandhi "Maneka Gandhi"), then [Minister of Women and Child Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Women_and_Child_Development_(India) "Ministry of Women and Child Development (India)") of India, raised a nation-wide petition to stop dog meat sale and consumption, specifically targeting Nagaland and Mizoram.[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-160) The issue caught public attention when she posted on Twitter on 30 June 2022 a photograph of dog trade in Nagaland, commenting: "This is illegal according to the laws of India and it cannot be allowed under the guise of culture."[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-161)[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-162) In [Nagaland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagaland "Nagaland"), pet lovers had launched a campaign to end Nagaland's dog meat trade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-163) Following Gandhi's remarks, the [Federation of Indian Animal Protection Organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Indian_Animal_Protection_Organisations "Federation of Indian Animal Protection Organisations") (FIAPO) formally requested the [Government of Nagaland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Nagaland "Government of Nagaland") to ban the consumption and trading of dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-164) On 3 July 2020, the Nagaland cabinet meeting passed the legislation for banning the sale and consumption of dog meat in the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-165)[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-166) The regulation was enacted the next day when the Chief Secretary of Nagaland, Temjen Toy, issued an order banning dog markets, the commercial import and trading of dogs, and commercial sale of dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-167)[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-168)

In November 2020, dog meat traders challenged the Nagaland's legislation at the Nagaland High Court under the case file _Neizevolie Kuotsu Alias Toni Kuotsu and ors v. State of Nagaland and ors_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-169) The legislation was momentarily withheld but without final decision.[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-170) In 2023, the case was put up at the [Gauhati High Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauhati_High_Court "Gauhati High Court") Kohima Bench, in which Justice Marli Vankung made a verdict on 2 June nullifying the government's legislation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:32-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-:32)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:42-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-:42) The judgement reads: "The prohibition of sale and consumption of dog meat, by the Executive branch of the Government, without there being any law passed by the legislature in relation to trade and consumption of dog meat is liable thus to be set aside, even though it was passed in accordance with a Cabinet decision."[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-2)[\[100\]](#refnote-:02)[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-173) The judgement further stated that the Chief Secretary is not an authority to make such legal matters,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:32-1)[\[171\]](#refnote-:32) and that selling and eating dog meat does not violate any law in India,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:42-1)[\[172\]](#refnote-:42) and the practice is "an accepted norm and food among people of Nagaland."[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-174)[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-175)

In Mizoram, dog meat is freely sold and eaten.[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-176)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-:22) According to the Mizoram Animal Slaughter Act 2013, dogs are classified alongside cattle as animals allowed for slaughter, meat trade and consumption.[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-178) The State Animal Welfare Board of the [Government of Mizoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Mizoram "Government of Mizoram") had challenged this classification. On 4 March 2020, the Mizoram State Assembly passed the Mizoram Animal Slaughter (Amendment) Bill, 2020, which removed dogs from animals permitted to be killed for meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-179)[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-180) The news was taken as prohibition of slaughter of dogs in the state,[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-181)[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-182) but is not the case as it merely implied lack of legislation on dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-183) In 2023, there were pressures from pet lovers to enforce the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals (Dog Breeding and Marketing) Rule 2017 that would ban dog meat sale, but were received with stern criticisms from the public.[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-184)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-1)[\[177\]](#refnote-:22)

In Meghalaya, killing of stray or domesticated dogs is a punishable offence since 9 September 2021, when the Animal Husbandry & Veterinary Department of the Government of Meghalaya issued an order that prohibited slaughter of dogs for any purpose.[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-185)

#### Indonesia

Indonesia is predominantly [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim "Muslim"), a faith which considers dog meat, along with pork, to be [haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram "Haram") (ritually unclean).[&#8202;](#ref-back-buzzfeed-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-buzzfeed) _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ has reported that in spite of this, dog meat consumption has been growing in popularity among Muslims and other ethnic groups in the country due to its cheap price and purported health or medicinal benefits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-187) However, dog meat consumption is rare in Indonesia and is not eaten by the most people.

Although reliable data on the dog meat trade is scarce, various welfare groups estimate that at least 1 million dogs are killed every year to be eaten.[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-188) On the resort island of Bali alone, between 60,000 and 70,000 dogs are slaughtered and eaten a year, in spite of lingering concerns about the spread of rabies following an outbreak of the disease there a few years ago, according to the Bali Animal Welfare Association.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-189) Marc Ching of the Animal Hope and Wellness Foundation claimed in 2017 that the treatment of dogs in Indonesia was the "most sadistic" out of anywhere they were killed for their meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-190) According to [Rappler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rappler "Rappler") and _[The Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Independent "The Independent")_, the slaughter process for dogs in [Tomohon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomohon "Tomohon"), [Sulawesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulawesi "Sulawesi") resulted in some of them being blowtorched alive.[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-191)[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-192)

The consumption of dog meat is often associated with the [Minahasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minahasa "Minahasa") culture of [North Sulawesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sulawesi "North Sulawesi"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-193) [Maluku culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_culture "Maluku culture"), [Toraja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toraja "Toraja") culture, various ethnic from [East Nusa Tenggara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Nusa_Tenggara "East Nusa Tenggara"), and the [Bataks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batak_(Indonesia) "Batak (Indonesia)") of northern Sumatra.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-194) The code for restaurants or vendors selling dog meat is "RW", an abbreviation for _rintek wuuk_ ( [Minahasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minahasan_languages "Minahasan languages") euphemism means "fine hair") or "B1" abbreviation for _biang_ ( [Batak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batak_languages "Batak languages") language for female dog or "bitch").

Popular Indonesian dog-meat dishes are Minahasan spicy meat dish called _[rica-rica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rica-rica "Rica-rica")_. Dog meat _rica-rica_ specifically called _rica-rica_ "RW" which stands for _Rintek Wuuk_ in the Minahasan language, which means "fine hair" as a euphemism referring for fine hair found in roasted dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-buzzfeed-1)[\[186\]](#refnote-buzzfeed) It is cooked as _Patong_ dish by Toraja people, and as _[Saksang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saksang "Saksang")_ "B1" (stands for _Biang_ which means "dog" or "bitch" in Batak dialect) by Batak people of North Sumatra. On [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java "Java"), there are several dishes made from dog meat, such as _sengsu_ ( _[tongseng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tongseng "Tongseng") asu_), [_sate_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satay "Satay") _jamu_ (lit. "medicinal satay"), and _kambing balap_ (lit. "racing goat"). _Asu_ is [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_language "Javanese language") for "dog".

Dog consumption in Indonesia gained attention during the [2012 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_Presidential_election "2012 United States Presidential election") when incumbent [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama") was pointed out by his opponent to have eaten dog meat served by his Indonesian stepfather [Lolo Soetoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lolo_Soetoro "Lolo Soetoro") when Obama was living in the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-buzzfeed-2)[\[186\]](#refnote-buzzfeed) Obama wrote about his experience of eating dog in his book _[Dreams of My Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dreams_of_My_Father "Dreams of My Father")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-YouTube1-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-YouTube1) and at the 2012 [White House Correspondents' Dinner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Correspondents'_Dinner "White House Correspondents' Dinner") joked about eating dog.[&#8202;](#ref-back-YouTube2-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-YouTube2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNN-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-CNN)

According to Lyn White of [Animals Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animals_Australia "Animals Australia"), the consumption of dog meat in Bali is not a long-held tradition. She said the meat first came from a Christian ethnic group coming to Bali, where a minority of the immigrants working in the hospitality industry have fuelled the trade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-198)

In June 2017, an investigative report discovered that tourists in [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali "Bali") are unknowingly eating dog meat sold by street vendors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-199)

#### Japan

Although the vast majority of Japanese do not eat dog meat, it has been reported that more than 100 outlets in the country have been selling it imported, mainly to foreign customers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-200)[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-201) In 675 AD, [Emperor Tenmu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_Tenmu "Emperor Tenmu") decreed a prohibition on its consumption during the 4th through 9th months of the year. According to _Meisan Shojiki Ōrai_ (名産諸色往来) published in 1760, the meat of wild dog was sold along with boar, [deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venison "Venison"), fox, [wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honsh%C5%AB_wolf "Honshū wolf"), [bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bear_hunting "Bear hunting"), [raccoon dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raccoon_dog "Raccoon dog"), [otter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otter "Otter"), [weasel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_weasel "Japanese weasel") and [cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cat_meat "Cat meat") in some regions of [Edo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edo "Edo").[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-202)

#### Mainland China

Most people in China do not consume dog meat and the practice is declining. Consuming dog meat is legal in [mainland China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_China "Mainland China") except for the city of Shenzhen,[&#8202;](#ref-back-The_Guardian-2)[\[92\]](#refnote-The_Guardian) and the Chinese Ministry of Agriculture has never issued quarantine procedures for slaughtering dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-203)[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-204)[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-205)

The eating of dog meat in China dates back to around 500BCE, and possibly even earlier. It has been suggested that wolves in southern China may have been domesticated as a source of meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT2009-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-NYT2009) [Mencius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mencius "Mencius") (372–289BCE) talked about dog meat as being an edible, dietary meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-207) It was reported in the early 2000s that the meat was thought to have medicinal properties, and had been popular in northern China during the winter, as it was believed to raise body temperature after consumption and promote warmth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-208)[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-209) Historical records have shown how in times of food scarcities (as in wartime situations), dogs could also be eaten as an emergency food source.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-210)

In modern times, the extent of dog consumption in China varies by region. Generally, it is most prevalent in some of the southern provinces, especially in [Canton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong "Guangdong"), Guangxi, [Yunnan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yunnan "Yunnan"), Sichuan, [Hubei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubei "Hubei") and [Anhui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anhui "Anhui"), as well as a few of the northern provinces such as [Henan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henan "Henan"), Shandong and [Jilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jilin "Jilin").[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-211) It was reportedly common in 2010 to find dog meat served in restaurants in Southern China, where dogs are reared on farms for consumption.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-212) In 2012, Chinese [netizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netizen "Netizen") and the Chinese police intercepted trucks transporting caged dogs to be slaughtered in localities such as [Chongqing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chongqing "Chongqing") and [Kunming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunming "Kunming").[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-213)

Since 2009,[&#8202;](#ref-back-O'Neil-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-O'Neil) [Yulin, Guangxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yulin,_Guangxi "Yulin, Guangxi"), has held a festival of eating dog meat that is reportedly a celebration of the [summer solstice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summer_solstice "Summer solstice"). Many Chinese citizens and activists were and are opposed to the festival. In 2014, the municipal government published a statement distancing itself from the festival, saying it was not a cultural tradition, but rather a commercial event held by restaurants and the public.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SMC-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-SMC) The festival in 2011 spanned 10 days, during which 15,000 dogs were consumed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-216) Estimates of the number of dogs eaten in 2015 for the festival ranged from as high as 10,000[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent2015-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-Independent2015)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC2015-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-BBC2015) to lower than 1,000 amid growing pressure at home and abroad to end it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-truthabout-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-truthabout)[&#8202;](#ref-back-dyingfestival-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-dyingfestival) Festival organizers state that only dogs bred specifically for consumption are used, while objectors say that some of the dogs purchased for slaughter and consumption are strays or stolen pets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent2015b-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-Independent2015b)[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-222) Some of the dogs at the festival are alleged to have been burnt or boiled alive[&#8202;](#ref-back-Asiaone-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-Asiaone) or beaten out of the belief that increased [adrenaline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrenaline "Adrenaline") circulating in the dog's body adds to the flavor of the meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent2015-1)[\[217\]](#refnote-Independent2015)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent2015b-1)[\[221\]](#refnote-Independent2015b) Other reports, however, state that there has been little evidence of those practices since 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dyingfestival-1)[\[220\]](#refnote-dyingfestival)[&#8202;](#ref-back-truthabout-1)[\[219\]](#refnote-truthabout)

Prior to the 2014 festival, eight dogs (and their two cages) sold for 1,150 yuan ($185) and six puppies for 1,200 yuan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-china.org-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-china.org) Prior to the 2015 festival, a protester bought 100 dogs for 7,000 yuan ($1,100; £710).[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC2015-1)[\[218\]](#refnote-BBC2015) The animal rights NGO Best Volunteer Centre commented that the city had more than 100 slaughterhouses, processing between 30 and 100 dogs a day. The Yulin Centre for Animal Disease Control and Prevention states the city has only eight dog slaughterhouses selling approximately 200 dogs, and this increases to about 2,000 dogs during the Yulin festival.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Huifeng-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-Huifeng) There have been several campaigns to stop the festival, with the first one reportedly having started among locals in China.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dyingfestival-2)[\[220\]](#refnote-dyingfestival) In 2016, a petition calling for an end to the festival garnered 11 million signatures in the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-226) More than 3 million people have also signed petitions against it on [Weibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sina_Weibo "Sina Weibo") (China's equivalent of Twitter).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent2015-2)[\[217\]](#refnote-Independent2015) Prior to the 2014 festival, doctors and nurses were ordered not to eat dog meat there, and local restaurants serving dog meat were ordered to cover the word "dog" on their signs and notices.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SMC-1)[\[215\]](#refnote-SMC) Reports in 2014 and 2016 have also suggested that the majority of Chinese on and offline disapprove of the festival.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-227)[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-228)[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-229)

The movement against the consumption of cat and dog meat was given added impetus by the formation of the Chinese Companion Animal Protection Network (CCAPN). Having expanded to more than 40 member societies, CCAPN began organizing protests against eating dog and cat meat in 2006, starting in Guangzhou and continuing in more than ten other cities following a positive response from the public.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Guangzhou_bans_eating_snakes--ban_helps_cats(Animal_People-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-Guangzhou_bans_eating_snakes--ban_helps_cats(Animal_People)-230) Before the [2008 Beijing Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Beijing_Olympics "2008 Beijing Olympics"), officials ordered dog meat to be taken off the menu at its 112 official Olympic restaurants to avoid offending visitors from various nations where the consumption of dog meat is taboo.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-231) In 2010, draft legislation was proposed to prohibit the consumption of dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-xinhua-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-xinhua) In 2010, the first draft proposal of it was introduced, with the rationale to protect animals from maltreatment. The legislation included a measure to jail people for up to 15 days for eating dog meat,[&#8202;](#ref-back-China_to_jail_people_for_up_to_15_days_who_eat_dog_(China_Daily-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-China_to_jail_people_for_up_to_15_days_who_eat_dog_(China_Daily)-233) but there were few expectations for it to be enforced.[&#8202;](#ref-back-xinhua-1)[\[232\]](#refnote-xinhua)

##### Decline

As of the early 21st century, dog meat consumption in China is declining.[&#8202;](#ref-back-China2-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-China2) In 2014, dog meat sales decreased by a third compared to 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Guardian-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-Guardian) It was reported that in 2015, one of the most popular restaurants in Guangzhou serving dog meat was closed after the local government tightened regulations; the restaurant had served dog meat dishes since 1963. Other restaurants that served dog and cat meat in the Yuancun and Panyu districts also stopped serving these dishes in 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-China-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-China) 8.6 million Chinese in 2016 also voted online for proposed legislation to end the consumption of dog and cat meat, but the legislation was not taken forward.[&#8202;](#ref-back-truthabout-2)[\[219\]](#refnote-truthabout)[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-237)

In April 2020, [Shenzhen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shenzhen "Shenzhen") became the first Chinese city to ban consumption and production of dog and cat meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-238) This came as part of a wider clampdown on the wildlife trade which was thought to be linked to [COVID-19 outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"). Citing examples of Hong Kong and Taiwan, the Shenzhen city government said, "Banning the consumption of dogs and cats and other pets is a common practice in developed countries... This ban also responds to the demand and spirit of human civilization".[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-239) The city of [Zhuhai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuhai "Zhuhai") followed suit in the same month with a similar ban.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Humane_Society_International-1)[\[240\]](#refnote-Humane_Society_International) These decisions were applauded by animal welfare groups such as [Humane Society International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humane_Society_International "Humane Society International").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Humane_Society_International-2)[\[240\]](#refnote-Humane_Society_International)[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-241)

In the same month, the Chinese Ministry of Agriculture said it considers dogs as "companion animals", not as livestock.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-242)[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-243)

#### Malaysia

Dog meat is not illegal in Malaysia, but its consumption is rare. The issue of dog meat was brought to light in 2013 after the Malaysian Independent Animal Rescue group received a report alleging that a restaurant in Kampung Melayu, [Subang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subang,_Selangor "Subang, Selangor") had dogs caged and tortured before slaughtering them for their meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-244)

#### North Korea

Most North Koreans do not consume dog meat, but its sale and consumption are allowed and endorsed by the government as part of [Korean culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_culture "Korean culture").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-:52)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:72-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-:72)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[13\]](#refnote-:0) Known as _dangogi_ ("sweet meat"), it is consumed as a delicacy, with tradition stating that it helps cool down body temperature during the summer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:82-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-:82) _[Dangogiguk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosintang "Bosintang")_ ("sweet meat soup" ) was declared a "regional intangible cultural heritage" of the DPRK in 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-1)[\[245\]](#refnote-:52)

_[Daily NK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_NK "Daily NK")_ reported that in early 2010, the [North Korean government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_North_Korea "Government of North Korea") included dog meat in its list of one hundred fixed prices, setting a fixed price of 500 [won](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korean_won "North Korean won") per kilogram.[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-248)

#### South Korea

Main article: [Dog meat consumption in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_meat_consumption_in_South_Korea "Dog meat consumption in South Korea")

_**Gaegogi**_ (개고기) literally means "dog meat" in Korean. The term itself is often mistaken as the term for [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_cuisine "Korean cuisine") [soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soup "Soup") made from dog meat, which is actually called _[bosintang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosintang "Bosintang")_ (보신탕; 補身湯, _Body nourishing soup_) (sometimes spelled "bo-shintang").

Estimates of the number of animals consumed vary widely. The [Humane Society International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humane_Society_International "Humane Society International") has estimated that 2 million[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-249) or possibly more than 2.5million dogs are reared on "dog meat farms" in South Korea (though, this number includes puppy mills for the pet industry).[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-250)[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-251) According to the [Korea Animal Rights Advocates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_Animal_Rights_Advocates "Korea Animal Rights Advocates") (KARA), approximately 780,000 to 1 million dogs are consumed per year in South Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-252) However, these numbers have been critiqued as not being based on actual data and having no scientific basis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-253)

Estimates of dog meat consumption is much lower when accounting for actual sales. In 2017 the Moran Market, which occupied 30–40% of dog meat market in the nation,[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-254) reported sales of about 20,000 dogs per year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-sports.khan.co.kr-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-sports.khan.co.kr) Numbers have further declined from these 2017 estimates and all the major markets have shutdown, including Moran Market.[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-256) According to the Ministry of Agriculture, Food and Rural Affairs, approximately 200 dog farms have been reported to be operating; though, the supply to the dog meat market is unclear as these farms also supply the pet industry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-257)

In 2022, the [Ministry for Food, Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_Food,_Agriculture,_Forestry_and_Fisheries_(South_Korea) "Ministry for Food, Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (South Korea)") of South Korea published a first official report called "Edible dog breeding and distribution survey". According to the report, as of February 2022, 521,121 dogs are reared in 1,156 dog meat farms and 388,000 dogs are consumed in 1,666 restaurants per year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-258)

Over the past 50 years, dog meat consumption has been declining as more people have been adopting dogs as pets. In a 2020 survey, 84% of the Korean population reported never having consumed dog meat nor having plans to ever do so.[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-259)

The most popular dish is the soup "boshingtang", a spicy stew meant to balance the body's heat during the summer months. Eating hot soups during the summer is thought to ensure good health by balancing one's " [qi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qi "Qi")", the believed vital energy of the body. Dog meat is believed by some to increase the body temperature, to induce sweating to keep one cool during the summer (the way of dealing with heat is called _heal heat with heat_ (이열치열, 以熱治熱, _i-yeol-chi-yeol_)). A 19th-century version of _gaejang-guk_ explains the preparation of the dish by boiling dog meat with vegetables such as [green onions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scallion "Scallion") and [chili pepper powder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_powder "Chili powder"). Variations of the dish contain [chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_(food) "Chicken (food)") and [bamboo shoots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamboo_shoot "Bamboo shoot").[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-260)

The sale of dog meat is illegal, but slaughter and consumption is legal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-261-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-261) However, 'brutal' slaughters of any animals is prohibited by the Animal Protection Law (동물보호법), and some people were fined for slaughter of dogs by this law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-262) The sale is disallowed because South Korean Food Sanitary Law (식품위생법) does not include dog meat as a legal food ingredient. The [Ministry of Food and Drug Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Food_and_Drug_Safety "Ministry of Food and Drug Safety") recognizes any edible product other than drugs as food.[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-263) In the capital city of [Seoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul "Seoul"), the sale of dog meat was outlawed by regulation on 21 February 1984, by classifying dog meat as "repugnant food" (혐오식품, 嫌惡食品, _hyeom-o sigpum_), but the regulation was not rigorously enforced except during the [1988 Seoul Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Seoul_Olympics "1988 Seoul Olympics"). In 2001, the Mayor of Seoul announced there would be no extra enforcement efforts to control the sale of dog meat during the [2002 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_FIFA_World_Cup "2002 FIFA World Cup"), which was partially hosted in Seoul. On 21 November 2018, the South Korean government closed the Taepyeong-dong complex in [Seongnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seongnam "Seongnam"), which served as the country's main dog slaughterhouse.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nov21-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-nov21)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nov21again-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-nov21again)

The primary dog breed raised for meat is a non-specific landrace, whose dogs are commonly named as _[Nureongi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nureongi "Nureongi")_ (누렁이) or _Hwangu_ (황구).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Podberscek-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-Podberscek)[&#8202;](#ref-back-edible-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-edible) Nureongi are not the only type of dog currently slaughtered for their meat in South Korea. In 2015, _The Korea Observer_ reported that many different pet breeds of dog are eaten in South Korea, including [Labrador Retrievers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labrador_Retriever "Labrador Retriever"), [Golden Retrievers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Retriever "Golden Retriever") and [Cocker Spaniels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocker_Spaniel "Cocker Spaniel"), and that the dogs slaughtered for their meat often include former pets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hyams-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-Hyams) Some of them have reportedly been stolen from family homes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-269)

There is some vocal group of Koreans (consisting of a number of animal welfare groups) who oppose the practice of eating dog meat. Some Koreans do not eat the meat, but feel that it is the right of others to do so.[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-270) A group of activists attempted to promote and publicize the consumption of dog meat worldwide during the run-up to the [2002 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_FIFA_World_Cup "2002 FIFA World Cup"), co-hosted by Japan and South Korea, which prompted retaliation from animal rights campaigners and prominent figures such as [Brigitte Bardot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigitte_Bardot "Brigitte Bardot") to denounce the practice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-271) Opponents of dog meat consumption in South Korea are critical of the eating of dog meat, as some dogs are beaten, burnt or hanged to make their meat more tender.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-272)[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-273) In more recent decades, such practices are being prosecuted by law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-274)[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-275)

Amidst the decline in dog meat consumption in contemporary Korea, a vocal group in Korea has critiqued the international outcry toward dog meat consumption as being hypocritical.[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-276) International animal rights activists have noted the hypocrisy, as well, given the horrific conditions under which factory farmed animals are raised in the West.[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-277) Some Korean citizens, as well as members of the international community, have pointed out that the nations from which most of the outcry has emerged have the highest per capita meat consumption on the planet, several-fold higher than that of South Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-278)[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-279)[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-280)

On 9 January 2024, the South Korean National Assembly passed a bill banning the breeding and slaughter of dogs for consumption, which will be starting in 2027 after a three-year grace period.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SK_Ban-2)[\[117\]](#refnote-SK_Ban)

#### Philippines

See also: [Philippine Animal Welfare Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Animal_Welfare_Society "Philippine Animal Welfare Society") and [Nita Hontiveros-Lichauco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nita_Hontiveros-Lichauco "Nita Hontiveros-Lichauco")

The killing of dogs as livestock has been banned in the Philippines since 1998 via [Republic Act No. 8485](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_laws "List of Philippine laws"), known as the [Animal Welfare Act of 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Welfare_Act_of_1998 "Animal Welfare Act of 1998"), with exemptions for dogs killed and eaten as part of indigenous rituals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-awa-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-awa) This was strengthened further by [Republic Act No. 10631](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_laws "List of Philippine laws") in 2013, which amended the 1998 law by criminalizing the abuse, maltreatment, cruelty, exploitation, abandonment, and killing of pet animals; while also mandating the creation of animal welfare enforcement officers in law enforcement agencies like the [Philippine National Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_National_Police "Philippine National Police") and the [National Bureau of Investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bureau_of_Investigation_(Philippines) "National Bureau of Investigation (Philippines)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ra10631-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-ra10631) [Republic Act No. 9842](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_laws "List of Philippine laws"), known as the Anti-Rabies Act of 2007, also includes specific provisions for the punishment of anyone found guilty of trading in dog meat, with a punishment of 1 to 4 years in prison and fines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pawsra9842-1)[\[113\]](#refnote-pawsra9842) Local laws banning the dog meat trade have also existed prior to the 1998 national ban.[&#8202;](#ref-back-manila-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-manila)[&#8202;](#ref-back-upi1-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-upi1) The illegal consumption of dog meat still continues in some regions in the Philippines, notably in the [Cordillera highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordillera_Central_(Luzon) "Cordillera Central (Luzon)") in northern [Luzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzon "Luzon") where it is traditional.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PDI2006-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-PDI2006-01) Despite the traditional practice, the consumption of dog meat can transmit a fatal viral disease called rabies,[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-286) where it claimed the lives of individuals reportedly consuming it, including an individual in Davao del Norte.[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-287) Nevertheless, the introduction and strengthening of laws against dog meat trade, changing public attitudes, and strict enforcement has significantly decreased the number of the dogs slaughtered for meat. As of 2020, less than 1% of the population of the Philippines have eaten dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-4paws-1)[\[39\]](#refnote-4paws)

Dog meat consumption was formerly widespread, particularly in the northern Philippines where dog meat stew, in general, acquired the [euphemistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphemism "Euphemism") name " **azucena**" (or " **asocena**") in the 1980s. It originates from a [play-on-words](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_play "Word play") on the Spanish term _azucena_, "lily", and its resemblance to a [portmanteau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portmanteau "Portmanteau") "asocena", from _aso_ ("dog") + _cena_, ("dinner"/"meal"). They were commonly eaten as _[pulutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulutan "Pulutan")_ (appetizers eaten with alcohol) by men, mainly due to the superstition that dog meat "warms the body" and functions as an [aphrodisiac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aphrodisiac "Aphrodisiac"). At the height of the dog meat trade prior to the 1990s, an estimated 500,000 dogs were killed every year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-regan-1)[\[38\]](#refnote-regan)[&#8202;](#ref-back-iwct-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-iwct)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Garcia-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-Garcia)

In 1981, the [diplomatic relations between the Philippines and the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations "Philippines–United Kingdom relations") were strained when the _[Sunday Mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunday_Mirror "Sunday Mirror")_ newspaper of London published front-page photos of dogs tied up for slaughter in the Philippines. This invoked [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher "Margaret Thatcher") and the [British parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_parliament "British parliament") to express their disapproval, which was informally conveyed to the [Philippine Foreign Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Foreign_Affairs "Department of Foreign Affairs") via the British Embassy. Though initially considered an internal affair, their communications became public, eliciting a response from [Assemblyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Batasang_Pambansa "Interim Batasang Pambansa") [Eddie Ilarde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Ilarde "Eddie Ilarde") of the [National Capital Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Manila "Metro Manila"), taking affront at suggestion that all Filipinos are cruel to dogs and eat dog meat. He clarified that while the dog meat trade does still happen, it is uncommon and prohibited by local laws in most parts of the country, including [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_City "Manila City").[&#8202;](#ref-back-og-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-og)[&#8202;](#ref-back-upi1-1)[\[284\]](#refnote-upi1) The issue sparked national interest on the dog meat trade and the inadequacy of the then existing laws against animal cruelty passed during the [American colonial period of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_colonial_period_of_the_Philippines "American colonial period of the Philippines"). Then president [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos "Ferdinand Marcos") ordered a study on the necessity of issuing a presidential decree prohibiting the sale and slaughter of household pets for commercial culinary purposes in 1982.[&#8202;](#ref-back-upi1982-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-upi1982) In the capital city of [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila "Manila"), Metro Manila Commission Ordinance 82-05 specifically prohibits the killing and selling of dogs for food.[&#8202;](#ref-back-manila-1)[\[283\]](#refnote-manila)

In 1998, the Animal Welfare Act was passed, which prohibits the killing of any animal other than cattle, pigs, goats, sheep, poultry, rabbits, [carabaos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carabao "Carabao"), horses, deer, and crocodiles – with exemptions for religious, cultural, research, public safety, and/or animal health reasons. It also specifically bans [dog fighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_fighting "Dog fighting") and [horse fighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_horse_fighting "Organized horse fighting").[&#8202;](#ref-back-PDI2006-1)[\[285\]](#refnote-PDI2006-01)[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-292)

The consumption of dog meat still continues in some areas illegally, especially in the [Cordillera highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordillera_Central_(Luzon) "Cordillera Central (Luzon)") of Luzon where indigenous tribes have legal exemptions for the consumption of dog meat during customary rituals. This [loophole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loophole "Loophole") has been taken advantage of by some restaurants which still sell dog meat outside of the customary ritual exemption.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PDI2006-2)[\[285\]](#refnote-PDI2006-01)

According to the [Animal Welfare Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Welfare_Institute "Animal Welfare Institute"), the illegal dog meat trade largely captures stray dogs from elsewhere in the country, with many of them having been people's pets. They are rounded up off the street for the dog meat trade and shipped to the [Benguet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benguet "Benguet") province without food or water while steel cans are forced onto their noses and their legs are tied behind their backs. Nearly half the dogs reportedly die before reaching their final destination. They are usually then killed via clubbing or having their throats cut, after which their fur is scorched off with a blow-torch and their bodies are dismembered.[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-293) According to a 2007 book co-authored by [Temple Grandin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Grandin "Temple Grandin"), dogs and other animals in some rural Philippine areas could risk getting beaten before slaughter, out of the belief it would create better meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-294) The province of [Benguet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benguet "Benguet") specifically allows cultural use of dog meat by indigenous people and acknowledges this might lead to limited commercial use.[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-295) In [Baguio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baguio "Baguio") and surrounding municipalities, it is regarded as an "open secret" tolerated by local law enforcement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PDI2006-3)[\[285\]](#refnote-PDI2006-01) Philippine news outlets [ABS-CBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABS-CBN "ABS-CBN") and [SunStar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SunStar "SunStar") stated in 2012 and 2017 that Korean nationals in [Baguio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baguio "Baguio") had been playing a role in the city's dog meat trade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ngalob-1)[\[36\]](#refnote-Ngalob)[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-296)[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-297)

In 2012, the [Criminal Investigation and Detection Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_Investigation_and_Detection_Group "Criminal Investigation and Detection Group") (CIDG) of the [Philippine National Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_National_Police "Philippine National Police") estimated that there were still around 290,000 dogs illegally butchered for food annually.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Yap-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-Yap) In 2013, 250 raids were carried out on restaurants suspected of serving dog meat in the Cordilleras by the Philippine National Police and the Animal Welfare Division of the Bureau of Animal Industry, in conjunction with the NGO [Animal Kingdom Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Kingdom_Foundation "Animal Kingdom Foundation"). Dog meat trade has decreased substantially since then.[&#8202;](#ref-back-4paws-2)[\[39\]](#refnote-4paws)

#### Singapore

The sale of dog meat is banned in Singapore.[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-299)\[ _[bettersourceneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\]

#### Taiwan

In 2001, the Taiwanese government imposed a ban on the sale of dog meat, due to both pressure from domestic animal welfare groups and a desire to improve international perceptions, and there were some protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-300-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-300) In 2007, another law was passed, significantly increasing the fines to sellers of the meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-chinapost-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-chinapost) According to [The Daily Meal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_Meal "The Daily Meal") in 2014, dog meat remained popular in Taiwan despite the laws, especially at smaller towns and villages.[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-302) Animal rights activists have accused the Taiwanese government of not prosecuting those who continue to slaughter and serve dog meat at restaurants.

In April 2017, Taiwan became the first country in East Asia to officially ban the consumption of dog and cat meat as well as jail time for those who torture and kill animals. The Animal Protection Act amendments approved by the Legislative Yuan aims to punish the sale, purchase or consumption of dog or cat meat with fines ranging from NT$50,000 to NT$2million. The amendments also stiffen punishment for those who intentionally harm animals to a maximum two years' imprisonment and fines of NT$200,000 to NT$2million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-303)

In October 2017, Taiwan's national legislature, known as Legislative Yuan, passed amendments to the country's Animal Protection Act which "bans the sale and consumption of dog and cat meat and of any food products that contain the meat or other parts of these animals."[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-304)

#### Timor-Leste

Dog meat consumption in Timor-Leste is rare and is not mainstream.[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-305)

#### Thailand

In 2014, Thailand passed the [Prevention of Animal Cruelty and Provision of Animal Welfare Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prevention_of_Animal_Cruelty_and_Provision_of_Animal_Welfare_Act "Prevention of Animal Cruelty and Provision of Animal Welfare Act").[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-306)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fuller-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-Fuller)

#### Uzbekistan

Dog meat has sometimes been used in Uzbekistan in the belief that it has medicinal properties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-308)[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-309)[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-310)[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-311)

#### Vietnam

Main article: [Dog meat consumption in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_meat_consumption_in_Vietnam "Dog meat consumption in Vietnam")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Vietnamese_dog_meat.jpg/250px-Vietnamese_dog_meat.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vietnamese_dog_meat.jpg) Dog meat in [Hanoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanoi "Hanoi"), Vietnam[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Dog_meat.jpg/250px-Dog_meat.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dog_meat.jpg) A dog meat platter found in a street market a few kilometres east of Hanoi, Vietnam[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Dog_meat_for_sale_in_a_market_in_Hanoi%2C_Vietnam_%286827793370%29.jpg/250px-Dog_meat_for_sale_in_a_market_in_Hanoi%2C_Vietnam_%286827793370%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dog_meat_for_sale_in_a_market_in_Hanoi,_Vietnam_(6827793370).jpg) Dog meat in Hanoi, Vietnam

Today, the majority of Vietnamese do not consume dog meat and the practice is on decline. In a 2021 survey, around 91% of respondents wanted dog meat to be banned. 95% of respondents also answered that dog meat consumption is not a part of Vietnamese culture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hong_Tuyet-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-Hong_Tuyet)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hodal2013-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-Hodal2013)[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-314)[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-315)

In urban areas, there are places that contain dog meat restaurants. Groups of customers, usually male, seated on mats, will spend their evenings sharing plates of dog meat and drinking alcohol. The consumption of dog meat can be part of a ritual usually occurring toward the end of the [lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month "Lunar month") for reasons of [astrology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrology "Astrology") and luck. Restaurants which mainly exist to serve dog meat may only open for the last half of the lunar month.[&#8202;](#ref-back-news.bbc.co.uk-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-news.bbc.co.uk)[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-317)

There used to be a large smuggling trade from Thailand to export dogs to Vietnam for human consumption.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hodal2013-1)[\[313\]](#refnote-Hodal2013) A concerted campaign between 2007 and 2014 by animal activists in Thailand, led by the [Soi Dog Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soi_Dog_Foundation "Soi Dog Foundation"), convinced authorities in both Thailand and Vietnam that the dog meat trade was a hindrance to efforts to tackle rabies in Southeast Asia. In 2014, Thailand introduced a new law against animal cruelty, which greatly increased penalties faced by dog smugglers. The trade had significantly diminished.[&#8202;](#ref-back-318-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-318)[&#8202;](#ref-back-news.bbc.co.uk-1)[\[316\]](#refnote-news.bbc.co.uk)[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-319)[&#8202;](#ref-back-320-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-320)[&#8202;](#ref-back-321-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-321)

There are indications that the desire to eat dog meat in Vietnam is declining.[&#8202;](#ref-back-China2-1)[\[234\]](#refnote-China2) Part of the decline is thought to be due to an increased number of Vietnamese people keeping dogs as pets, as their incomes have risen in the past few decades.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCNews2018-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-BBCNews2018)[&#8202;](#ref-back-news.bbc.co.uk-2)[\[316\]](#refnote-news.bbc.co.uk)

### Europe

#### Austria

Section 6, Paragraph 2 of the law for the protection of animals prohibits the killing of dogs and cats for purposes of consumption as food or for other products.[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-323)

#### Switzerland

In 2012, the Swiss newspaper _[Tages-Anzeiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tages-Anzeiger "Tages-Anzeiger")_ reported that dogs, as well as cats, are eaten regularly by a few farmers in rural areas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-thelocal.ch-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-thelocal.ch)[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-325)[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-326) Commercial slaughter and sale of dog meat is banned, but farmers are allowed to slaughter dogs for personal consumption.

In his 1979 book _[Unmentionable Cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unmentionable_Cuisine "Unmentionable Cuisine")_, Calvin Schwabe described a Swiss dog meat recipe, _gedörrtes Hundefleisch_, served as paper-thin slices, as well as smoked dog ham, _Hundeschinken_, which is prepared by salting and drying raw dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchwabe1979[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidSiBntk9jGmoCpgPA173_173]-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchwabe1979[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidSiBntk9jGmoCpgPA173_173])

It is illegal in Switzerland to commercially produce food made from dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-328)

#### United Kingdom

Although the commercial trade of dog meat is illegal, it is still currently legal in the United Kingdom to consume dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-329)

#### France

In France, butcher shops selling dog meat were open all around the country until c.1910.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

In his poem " [Alcools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcools "Alcools")", [Guillaume Apollinaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillaume_Apollinaire "Guillaume Apollinaire") mentions a butcher who sells dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-330)

### Oceania

#### Australia

Each Australian state or territory has its own regulation, but all have laws either making it illegal to eat dog meat or to kill a dog for consumption. It is also prohibited to sell dog meat based on meat processing standards and codes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-331)

#### Tonga

The consumption of domestic dog meat is commonplace in Tonga, and has also been noted in expatriate Tongan communities in New Zealand, Australia, and the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-332)[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-333)

## Other dog products

### Central Asia

According to [Eurasianet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasianet "Eurasianet"), dog fat is seen as a well-established would-be treatment for tuberculosis in parts of Central Asia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Imanaliyeva-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-Imanaliyeva) The fat has reportedly been used as a folk remedy for [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19 "COVID-19") in Uzbekistan[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-335) and Kyrgyzstan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Imanaliyeva-1)[\[334\]](#refnote-Imanaliyeva)

### Poland

Eating dog meat is taboo in Polish culture. However, since the 16th century, fat from various animals, including dogs, was used as part of [folk medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_medicine "Folk medicine"), and since the 18th century dog fat has had a reputation as being beneficial for the lungs. While making lard, or [smalec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smalec "Smalec"), out of dogs' fat is currently discouraged in the country,[&#8202;](#ref-back-telegraph.co.uk-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-telegraph.co.uk) this practice continues in some rural areas, especially [Lesser Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Poland "Lesser Poland").[&#8202;](#ref-back-337-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-337)[&#8202;](#ref-back-338-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-338)

In 2009, Polish prosecutors reportedly found that a farm near [Częstochowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cz%C4%99stochowa "Częstochowa") was overfeeding dogs to be rendered down into lard.[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-339)[&#8202;](#ref-back-telegraph.co.uk-1)[\[336\]](#refnote-telegraph.co.uk) According to Grażyna Zawada, from _[Gazeta Wyborcza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazeta_Wyborcza "Gazeta Wyborcza")_, there were farms in Częstochowa, Kłobuck, and in the Radom area, and in the decade from 2000 to 2010 six people producing dog lard were found guilty of breaching animal welfare laws and sentenced to jail.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Szumińska-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-Szumińska)\[ _[failed verification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] However, the _[Krakow Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krakow_Post "Krakow Post")_ reported that a man who had admitted to stealing and killing dogs for lard in 2009 at [Wieliczka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wieliczka "Wieliczka") was found not guilty of any crimes by the court, who ruled that the dogs had been slaughtered humanely for culinary purposes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-341-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-341) As of 2014, there have been new cases prosecuted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-342-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-342)\[ _[failed verification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\]

### South Korea

See also: [Dog meat consumption in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_meat_consumption_in_South_Korea "Dog meat consumption in South Korea")

Gaesoju (개소주; 개燒酒) also known as dog wine, is a mixed drink containing dog meat and other Chinese medicine ingredients such as ginger, chestnut, and jujube to act primarily as a powerful sex drive booster for men, though it is also used to get rid of colds.

It is produced by putting a slaughtered dog into a chicken plucker to remove the fur before being placed into a pressure cooker for 6–19 hours with herbs until it is a dark liquid and bottled as a drink. Women in South Korea produce homemade gaesoju for their husbands as a bedroom gift, or they purchase commercial gaesoju from special markets.

In terms of popularity, there is a South Korean song which mentions a wife turning a dog into gaesoju for her husband[&#8202;](#ref-back-343-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-343) and popularity of Gaesoju as a sex tonic is rising compared to dog meat sales.[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-344)

## Dog breeds used for meat

The [Nureongi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nureongi "Nureongi") in Korea is most often used as a livestock dog, raised for its meat, and not commonly kept as pets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Morris_Dogs_pp._585-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-Morris_Dogs_pp._585)[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-346)

The [Tosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tosa_(dog) "Tosa (dog)"), or Japanese Fighting Dog is replacing older breeds or mutts in South Korea. The Tosa is not commonly a pet and is banned in multiple countries; it is also very lean with a little bit of fat, making it ideal for meat production. Currently only government-approved dog farms in Korea raise Tosa for meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-347)

[Tang Dogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_Dog "Tang Dog") are prized as a companion and watch dog but are also occasionally used for hunting and as meat dogs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-348)[&#8202;](#ref-back-349-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-349)

The [Chow Chow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chow_Chow "Chow Chow") was also known as "Chinese Edible-Dog" because after the Han dynasty collapsed, they were fattened and bred with Chinese breeds for meat. Today, Chinese dog farms still raise Chow Chow for the purpose of eating; black coated ones are valued due to their taste when fried, while the rest are typically turned into stews.[&#8202;](#ref-back-350-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-350)

The [Xoloitzcuintli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xoloitzcuintli "Xoloitzcuintli"), or Mexican hairless dog, is one of several breeds of [hairless dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hairless_dog "Hairless dog") and has been used as a historical source of food for the [Aztec Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztec_Empire "Aztec Empire").[&#8202;](#ref-back-About_THE_XOLOITZCUINTLE-1)[\[20\]](#refnote-About_THE_XOLOITZCUINTLE)

The extinct [Hawaiian Poi Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Poi_Dog "Hawaiian Poi Dog") and [Polynesian Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_Dog "Polynesian Dog") were breeds of [pariah dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pariah_dog "Pariah dog") used by [Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiian "Native Hawaiian") as a spiritual protector of children and as a source of food.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Titcomb_1969-1)[\[42\]](#refnote-Titcomb_1969)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ellis_1839-1)[\[43\]](#refnote-Ellis_1839)

The extinct [Tahitian Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitian_Dog "Tahitian Dog") was a food source, and served by high ranking chiefs to the early European explorers who visited the islands. Captain [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook "James Cook") and his crew developed a taste for the dog, with Cook noting, "For tame Animals they have Hogs, Fowls, and Dogs, the latter of which we learned to Eat from them, and few were there of us but what allow'd that a South Sea dog was next to an English Lamb."[&#8202;](#ref-back-Salmond2003-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-Salmond2003)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Parkinson-0)[\[352\]](#refnote-Parkinson)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Holland-0)[\[353\]](#refnote-Holland)

## World Dog Alliance

**World Dog Alliance**, founded in 2014, is a Hong kong based non profit international animal protection and [charity organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charitable_organization "Charitable organization")[&#8202;](#ref-back-354-0)[\[354\]](#refnote-354)[&#8202;](#ref-back-355-0)[\[355\]](#refnote-355) operating in the United States, [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France "France"), [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan "Japan"), and [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong "Hong Kong").[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[356\]](#refnote-356)[&#8202;](#ref-back-357-0)[\[357\]](#refnote-357)[&#8202;](#ref-back-358-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-358) [Horiike Hiroshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horiike_Hiroshi "Horiike Hiroshi") founded the World Dog Alliance in December 2014, aimed at fights to end the dog meat trade through legislation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-359-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-359)[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-360) The WDA claims support from legislators in the U.S.,[&#8202;](#ref-back-361-0)[\[361\]](#refnote-361) China, U.K.,[&#8202;](#ref-back-362-0)[\[362\]](#refnote-362)[&#8202;](#ref-back-363-0)[\[363\]](#refnote-363) Indonesia,[&#8202;](#ref-back-364-0)[\[364\]](#refnote-364) South Korea,[&#8202;](#ref-back-365-0)[\[365\]](#refnote-365) Japan, Norway, Sweden and others for its proposed international agreement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-366-0)[\[366\]](#refnote-366)

### Key campaigns and milestones

- In 2017, Taiwan revised its Animal Protection Act to outlaw dog and cat meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-367-0)[\[367\]](#refnote-367)
- In 2018, the U.S. "Farm Bill" included a ban on the slaughter and consumption of dogs and cats for human food.[&#8202;](#ref-back-368-0)[\[368\]](#refnote-368)
- In 2020, cities such as Shenzhen[&#8202;](#ref-back-369-0)[\[369\]](#refnote-369) and Zhuhai[&#8202;](#ref-back-370-0)[\[370\]](#refnote-370) introduced bans on dog and cat meat consumption.
- In 2021, Vietnam's Hoi An implemented initiatives to phase out dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-371-0)[\[371\]](#refnote-371) Restaurants serving dog meat would be detrimental to Hoi An's standing as a popular tourist destination. As a result, the city started to phase out dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-372-0)[\[372\]](#refnote-372)
- In 2023, the WDA publicly congratulated Jakarta for banning the dog and cat meat trade as part of its local food regulation reforms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-373-0)[\[373\]](#refnote-373)
- In 2024, South Korea's parliament passed bill to ban eating dog meat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-374-0)[\[374\]](#refnote-374)
- In 2024, the U.S. Congress included language referencing the International Agreement in its Appropriations Bill.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AP_News-0)[\[375\]](#refnote-AP_News)
- The United States passed the "U.S. Appropriations Bill 2024" which included language on the "International Agreement to Prohibit the Eating of Dogs and Cats", championed by World Dog Alliance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AP_News-1)[\[375\]](#refnote-AP_News)

[AS 2005 6043](http://www.admin.ch/ch/d/as/2005/6043.pdf) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20060518151132/http://www.admin.ch/ch/d/as/2005/6043.pdf) 18 May 2006 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine "Wayback Machine") (PDF) = [SR 817.022.108](http://www.admin.ch/ch/d/sr/c817_022_108.html) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20130107095100/http://www.admin.ch/ch/d/sr/c817_022_108.html) 7 January 2013 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine "Wayback Machine"); contains a list of animal species allowed for commercial food production.

     Previously, Art. 121 _[Lebensmittelverordnung (LMV)](https://www.admin.ch/opc/de/classified-compilation/19981860/200407010000/817.02.pdf) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201024133155/https://www.admin.ch/opc/de/classified-compilation/19981860/200407010000/817.02.pdf) 24 October 2020 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine "Wayback Machine") vom 1. März 1995_. – See also the ruling of [Schweizerisches Bundesgericht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_courts_of_Switzerland "Federal courts of Switzerland") of 24 September 1913, [BGE 39 I 407](http://www.servat.unibe.ch/dfr/pdf/c1039407.pdf) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20210929195528/https://www.servat.unibe.ch/dfr/pdf/c1039407.pdf) 29 September 2021 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine "Wayback Machine") _(Kassationsbeschwerde gegen ein Urteil des Amtsgerichts Bucheggberg-Kriegstetten)._ This ruling confirmed Art. 24 of the _Bundesrätliche Verordnung über das Schlachten, die Fleischschau und den Verkehr mit Fleisch und Fleischwaren_ of 29 January 1909: "Der Verkehr mit Hunde- und Katzenfleisch und mit daraus hergestellten Waren ist verboten".

- Barr, James, Capt. (1836). Correct And Authentic Narrative of the Florida War with a Description of MAJ. Dade's Massacre, and an Account of the Extreme Suffering, For Want of Provisions of the Army-Having Been Obliged to Eat Horses' and Dogs' Flesh, etc. New York: J. Narine, Printer, 11 Wall St.
- Yong-Geun Ann, PhD. [_Dog Meat_](https://web.archive.org/web/20110722132717/http://wolf.ok.ac.kr/~annyg/english/) (in Korean and English). Hyoil Book Publishing Company. Archived from [the original](http://wolf.ok.ac.kr/~annyg/english/) on 22 July 2011. Retrieved 18 September 2009. (contains some recipes)
- Dressler, Uwe; Alexander Neumeister (1 May 2003). _Der Kalte Hund_ (in German). Dresden: IBIS-Ed. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-3-8330-0650-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-3-8330-0650-0 "Special:BookSources/978-3-8330-0650-0").
- Sandburg, Carl. (1970). _Abraham Lincoln, The Prairie Years and The War Years._ Illustrated Edition. The Reader's Digest Association, Pleasantville, New York; The Reader's Digest Association LTD, Montreal, Canada.
- Zawada, Grazyna (28 October 2010). ["Szesc psow w sloiku"](http://wyborcza.pl/duzyformat/1,127291,8720913,Szesc_psow_w_sloiku.html). _[Gazeta Wyborcza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazeta_Wyborcza "Gazeta Wyborcza")_ (in Polish). Retrieved 26 March 2014.
- Aisin Gioro, Ulhicun; Jin, Shi. ["Manchuria from the Fall of the Yuan to the rise of the Manchu State (1368–1636)"](http://www.ritsumei.ac.jp/acd/cg/lt/rb/601/601PDF/aisin.pdf)(PDF). Retrieved 10 March 2014.
